````math
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[\hat{r}_{0}]-\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0})\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}]-\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{0}).
``` |  |

We begin by inserting and subtracting $`\hat{r}_{0}`$ terms into the
definition of the performance gap:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[r_{0}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[r_{0}^{*}]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[\hat{r}_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}]\big)+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[r_{0}^{*}-\hat{r}_{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r_{0}^{*}].`$ |  |

Applying the DPO optimality condition to the first term yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[r_{0}^{*}-\hat{r}_{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r_{0}^{*}]+\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0})-\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{0}).`$ |  | (15) |

We next apply the baseline-cancellation identity
Eq. [11](#A3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ Shorthand Notation and a Baseline-Cancellation Identity. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
to refine the two expectation terms. For the first term:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[r^{*}_{0}-\hat{r}_{0}]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[r^{*}_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[r^{*}_{0}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*}}[\hat{r}_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\hat{r}_{0}]\big)+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[r^{*}_{0}-\hat{r}_{0}]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{r^{*}_{0}}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[r^{*}_{0}-\hat{r}_{0}]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{r^{*}_{0}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[r^{*}_{0}-\hat{r}_{0}],`$ |  | (16) |

Similarly, for the second term:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r^{*}_{0}]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[\hat{r}_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\hat{r}_{0}]\big)-\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[r^{*}_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[r^{*}_{0}]\big)+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r^{*}_{0}]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{r^{*}_{0}}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r^{*}_{0}]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}-\Delta^{r^{*}_{0}}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\hat{r}_{0}-r^{*}_{0}].`$ |  | (17) |

Substituting
Eq. [16](#A3.E16 "Equation 16 ‣ Decomposition of the Performance Gap. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
and
Eq. [17](#A3.E17 "Equation 17 ‣ Decomposition of the Performance Gap. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
into
Eq. [15](#A3.E15 "Equation 15 ‣ Decomposition of the Performance Gap. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
and observing that the baseline terms cancel exactly, we obtain the
final decomposition:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}-\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}\big]+\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0})-\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{0}).
``` |  | (18) |

This decomposition forms the foundation for subsequent statistical
analysis of the performance gap.

### C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition

In this section we control the two principal terms in the performance
bound
Eq. [18](#A3.E18 "Equation 18 ‣ Decomposition of the Performance Gap. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
namely
$`\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}\big]`$
and
$`\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}-\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}\big]`$.
Our strategy is to decompose the expectation of the absolute difference
$`|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|`$ into contributions from a
*good region*, where the pairwise reward differences are uniformly
bounded, and a complementary *bad region*, which captures rare tail
events.

##### Preliminaries.

We recall the coupled–expectation shorthand

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}[\cdot]\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\,\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x),\;y^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}(\cdot\mid x)}[\cdot],
``` |  |

and the pairwise differences of interest:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}(x,y,y^{\prime})`$ | $`\displaystyle:=r_{0}^{*}(x,y)-r_{0}^{*}(x,y^{\prime})\;=\;\beta\log\frac{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}-\beta\log\frac{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}(y^{\prime}\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y^{\prime}\mid x)},`$ |  | (19) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}(x,y,y^{\prime})`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\hat{r}_{0}(x,y)-\hat{r}_{0}(x,y^{\prime})\;=\;\beta\log\frac{\pi_{1}(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}-\beta\log\frac{\pi_{1}(y^{\prime}\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y^{\prime}\mid x)}.`$ |  | (20) |

We also use the standard concentrability coefficient of a policy $`\pi`$
with respect to the baseline $`\pi_{0}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\pi}:=\mathbb{E}_{x}\,\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\!\Big[\frac{\pi(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\,\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)}\!\Big[\Big(\frac{\pi(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}\Big)^{2}\Big].
``` |  |

##### Good/Bad region decomposition.

Fix a threshold $`\eta>0`$, and define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
G\;:=\;\{\,|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|\leq\eta,\;|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|\leq\eta\,\},\qquad B\;:=\;G^{\complement}\;=\;\{\,|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|>\eta\;\text{ or }\;|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|>\eta\,\}.
``` |  |

Let $`D:=\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}`$. Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\big]\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\;+\;\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\,\mathbb{I}_{B}\big].
``` |  | (21) |

We now bound the two contributions on the right-hand side.

###### Lemma C.2 (Change of measure with concentrability).

For any nonnegative measurable function $`f`$, the coupled expectation
under $`(\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0})`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}[f]\;\leq\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[f^{2}]\Big)^{1/2},\qquad C_{\pi}\;:=\;\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\,\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}\right)^{2}\right].
``` |  | (22) |

###### Proof.

We expand the coupled expectation explicitly:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}[f]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\,\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{1}^{*}(\cdot\mid x)\\
y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)\end{subarray}}\!\big[f(x,y,y^{\prime})\big].
``` |  |

Reweighting the distribution of $`y`$ from $`\pi_{1}^{*}`$ to
$`\pi_{0}`$ introduces the ratio

|     |                                                            |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       w(x,y)\;:=\;\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}.
       ```                                                         |     |

Hence

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}[f]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\,\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)\\
y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)\end{subarray}}\!\big[w(x,y)\,f(x,y,y^{\prime})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[w\,f].
``` |  |

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality on the joint law
$`(x,y,y^{\prime})\sim\mu\otimes\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{0}`$ gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[w\,f]\;\leq\;\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[w^{2}]\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[f^{2}]\Big)^{1/2}.
``` |  |

Finally, observe that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[w^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\,\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\pi_{1}^{*}(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}\right)^{2}\right]=C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}.
``` |  |

Substituting this expression completes the proof of
Eq. [22](#A3.E22 "Equation 22 ‣ Lemma C.2 (Change of measure with concentrability). ‣ Good/Bad region decomposition. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").
∎

##### Bounding the bad–region term.

We now derive a usable bound on the bad–region contribution. Starting
from
Lemma [C.2](#A3.Thmtheorem2 "Lemma C.2 (Change of measure with concentrability). ‣ Good/Bad region decomposition. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
and choosing the test function $`f=|D|\mathbb{I}_{B}`$, we immediately
obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\,\mathbb{I}_{B}\big]\;\leq\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[D^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{B}]\Big)^{1/2}.
``` |  | (23) |

Thus the task reduces to bounding the second moment of $`D`$ restricted
to the bad region.

To proceed, we invoke Hölder’s inequality (equivalently, a second
application of Cauchy–Schwarz), which separates the event probability
from the higher–order moment:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[D^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{B}]\;\leq\;\big(\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}(B)\big)^{1/2}\,\big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[D^{4}]\big)^{1/2}.
``` |  | (24) |

Here the first factor controls the likelihood of landing in the bad
region, while the second factor controls the size of the reward
differences when such events occur.

The probability of the bad region itself can be bounded via a simple
union bound. Since $`B`$ is the event that either
$`|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|`$ or $`|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|`$ exceeds the
threshold $`\eta`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}(B)\;\leq\;\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\!\big(|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|>\eta\big)\;+\;\mathbb{P}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\!\big(|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|>\eta\big).
``` |  | (25) |

It remains to bound the fourth moment of
$`D=\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}`$. By the elementary
inequality $`(a-b)^{4}\leq 8(a^{4}+b^{4})`$, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[D^{4}]\;\leq\;8\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|^{4}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|^{4}]\right).
``` |  | (26) |

Finally, by combining
Eq. [23](#A3.E23 "Equation 23 ‣ Bounding the bad–region term. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")–Eq. [26](#A3.E26 "Equation 26 ‣ Bounding the bad–region term. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
and invoking the standard fourth-moment bound provided in Lemma J.2 of
([Huang et al., 2025](#bib.bib13)), we obtain the following compact
estimate.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\,\mathbb{I}_{B}\big]\;\lesssim\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Gamma\,\Upsilon^{1/4},
``` |  | (27) |

where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Gamma:=\log\!\big(C_{\pi_{0}/\pi_{1};\beta}\big)+\log\!\big(C_{\pi_{0}/\pi_{1,\beta}^{*};\beta}\big),\qquad\Upsilon:=\mathbb{P}(|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|>\eta)+\mathbb{P}(|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|>\eta).
``` |  |

In words, the bad–region contribution is controlled by three factors:
the concentrability of the comparator policy $`\pi_{1}^{*}`$, a
logarithmic moment term $`\Gamma`$ stemming from higher–order tail
bounds, and the tail probability $`\Upsilon`$, raised to the quarter
power.

##### Bounding the good–region term.

Applying
Lemma [C.2](#A3.Thmtheorem2 "Lemma C.2 (Change of measure with concentrability). ‣ Good/Bad region decomposition. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
with $`f=|D|\mathbb{I}_{G}`$ gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[|D|\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\;\leq\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\big[D^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\Big)^{1/2}.
``` |  | (28) |

Note that on $`G`$ both $`|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}|`$ and
$`|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|`$ are clipped by $`\eta`$, so the remaining
quantity is a bounded squared error that will be controlled by an
empirical process argument.

##### Putting the pieces together.

From
Eq. [21](#A3.E21 "Equation 21 ‣ Good/Bad region decomposition. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
Eq. [27](#A3.E27 "Equation 27 ‣ Bounding the bad–region term. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
and
Eq. [28](#A3.E28 "Equation 28 ‣ Bounding the good–region term. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}^{*},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\,|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|\,\big]\;\lesssim\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\big[|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\Big)^{1/2}\;+\;C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}\,\Gamma\,\Upsilon^{1/4}.
``` |  | (29) |

By the same argument with $`\pi_{1}`$ in place of $`\pi_{1}^{*}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{0}}\!\big[\,|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-|\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|\,\big]\;\lesssim\;C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\big[|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\Big)^{1/2}\;+\;C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2}\,\Gamma\,\Upsilon^{1/4}.
``` |  | (30) |

Finally, using $`\mathbb{E}[X]\leq\mathbb{E}[|X|]`$ and substituting
Eq. [29](#A3.E29 "Equation 29 ‣ Putting the pieces together. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")–Eq. [30](#A3.E30 "Equation 30 ‣ Putting the pieces together. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
into
Eq. [18](#A3.E18 "Equation 18 ‣ Decomposition of the Performance Gap. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we obtain the intermediate performance–gap bound

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\lesssim\;`$ | $`\displaystyle(C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}+C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2})\,\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\big[|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|^{2}\,\mathbb{I}_{G}\big]\Big)^{1/2}\;+\;(C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}+C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2})\,\Gamma\,\Upsilon^{1/4}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0})\;-\;\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}\|\pi_{0}).`$ |  | (31) |

The first (“good–region”) term will be bounded by a uniform convergence
argument, while the tail term $`\Gamma\,\Upsilon^{1/4}`$ is controlled
by choosing $`\eta`$ and the clipping level in the empirical loss
appropriately. This completes the good/bad region analysis used to bound
the differences of reward deltas.

### C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound

The bound derived in
Eq. [31](#A3.Ex54 "In Putting the pieces together. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
depends on the population-level squared difference
$`\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}[|\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}-\Delta^{\hat{r}_{0}}|^{2}\mathbb{I}_{G}]`$.
In this section, we establish a connection between this population
quantity and the empirical performance of the learned policy
$`\pi_{1}`$, which is obtained through Empirical Risk Minimization
(ERM). To achieve this, we leverage classical tools from uniform
convergence theory, which allow us to translate the ERM guarantee into a
high-probability control of the population-level error term.

##### The ERM Solution and Its Empirical Loss.

To simplify notation, we index the pairwise difference operator by a
policy $`\pi`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta^{\pi}(x,y,y^{\prime}):=\beta\log\frac{\pi(y\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y\mid x)}-\beta\log\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid x)}{\pi_{0}(y^{\prime}\mid x)}.
``` |  |

From the reward identity in
Eq. [13](#A3.E13 "Equation 13 ‣ A Reward Identity via Entropy Regularization. ‣ C.2 A Reward Identity and the Performance Gap Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we note that $`\Delta^{r_{0}^{*}}=\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}`$. The
policy $`\pi_{1}`$ is defined as the ERM solution over the class
$`\Pi`$, minimizing the squared loss with respect to the oracle
$`\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}`$. Since the oracle belongs to the hypothesis class,
$`\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}\in\Pi`$, the empirical risk of $`\pi_{1}`$ must be
no larger than that of $`\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}`$, which is zero. Formally,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{(x,y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}\big(\Delta^{\pi_{1}}(x,y,y^{\prime})-\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}(x,y,y^{\prime})\big)^{2}\;\leq\;\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{(x,y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}\big(\Delta^{\pi}-\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}\big)^{2}\;\leq\;0.
``` |  | (32) |

##### A Uniform Convergence Bound via Bernstein’s Inequality.

To generalize the zero empirical loss to the population setting, we
appeal to Bernstein’s inequality together with a union bound over the
policy class. Let
$`\mathcal{D}_{0}=\{(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n}`$ be i.i.d.
samples with $`x_{i}\sim\mu`$ and
$`y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\sim\pi_{0}(\cdot\mid x_{i})`$.

To apply concentration inequalities, we require bounded variables. For
any policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$, we define the clipped squared loss

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Z_{i}(\pi):=\big(\Delta^{\pi}(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})-\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\big)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}\big\{|\Delta^{\pi}(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})|\leq B,\;|\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})|\leq B\big\},
``` |  |

where the clipping threshold $`B=B_{n,\rho}`$ will be chosen later. The
empirical and population losses are then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{L}_{n}(\pi):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(\pi),\qquad L(\pi):=\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}(\pi)].
``` |  |

On the clipping event, both differences are bounded by $`B`$, hence
$`|\Delta^{\pi}-\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}|\leq 2B`$. Thus the clipped
loss is uniformly bounded as

|     |                                         |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       0\;\leq\;Z_{i}(\pi)\;\leq\;M:=(2B)^{2}.
       ```                                      |     | (33) |

Because $`\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}\in\Pi`$ and by construction
$`Z_{i}(\pi_{1,\beta}^{*})\equiv 0`$, the ERM property
Eq. [32](#A3.E32 "Equation 32 ‣ The ERM Solution and Its Empirical Loss. ‣ C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
implies

|     |                                                               |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \hat{L}_{n}(\pi_{1})\;\leq\;\hat{L}_{n}(\pi_{1,\beta}^{*})=0.
       ```                                                            |     |

Since the clipped loss is always nonnegative, it follows that

|     |                         |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \hat{L}_{n}(\pi_{1})=0.
       ```                      |     | (34) |

For any fixed $`\pi`$, the variables $`\{Z_{i}(\pi)\}_{i=1}^{n}`$ are
i.i.d. and bounded in $`[0,M]`$ by
Eq. [33](#A3.E33 "Equation 33 ‣ A Uniform Convergence Bound via Bernstein’s Inequality. ‣ C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").
Bernstein’s inequality then yields, for all $`t>0`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}\ \big(L(\pi)-\hat{L}_{n}(\pi)\geq t\big)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-\frac{nt^{2}}{2\,\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i}(\pi))+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right).
``` |  |

Using the variance bound $`\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i}(\pi))\leq ML(\pi)`$, a
standard fixed–point calculation leads to the empirical Bernstein form:
with probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
L(\pi)\ \leq\ 2\,\hat{L}_{n}(\pi)\ +\ c\,\frac{M\log(1/\delta)}{n},
``` |  | (35) |

for a universal constant $`c>0`$.

Applying
Eq. [114](#A6.E114 "Equation 114 ‣ 3. Performance Bound for a Single Policy. ‣ F.3.2 Step 1: Bounding the Loss over an 𝜀-Net ‣ F.3 Uniform Convergence Bound for the Linear Softmax Model ‣ Appendix F Proof of Theorem : Performance Guarantees for Linear Softmax Models ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
with $`\delta=\rho/|\Pi|`$ and union–bounding over all $`\pi\in\Pi`$, we
obtain that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\forall\,\pi\in\Pi:\qquad L(\pi)\ \leq\ 2\,\hat{L}_{n}(\pi)\ +\ c\,\frac{M\log(|\Pi|/\rho)}{n}.
``` |  | (36) |

Since
Eq. [36](#A3.E36 "Equation 36 ‣ A Uniform Convergence Bound via Bernstein’s Inequality. ‣ C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
holds uniformly over $`\Pi`$, it applies in particular to the ERM policy
$`\pi_{1}`$. Combining with
Eq. [34](#A3.E34 "Equation 34 ‣ A Uniform Convergence Bound via Bernstein’s Inequality. ‣ C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
yields

|     |                                                   |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       L(\pi_{1})\ \leq\ c\,\frac{M\log(|\Pi|/\rho)}{n}.
       ```                                                |     |

Substituting $`M=(2B)^{2}`$ gives the population-level bound

|     |                                                        |     |      |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       L(\pi_{1})\ \lesssim\ \frac{B^{2}\log(|\Pi|/\rho)}{n}.
       ```                                                     |     | (37) |

Finally, we specify the clipping level

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
B_{n,\rho}:=\log\!\Big(\frac{2n\,C_{\mathrm{loss}}\,|\Pi|}{\rho}\Big).
``` |  |

This selection is motivated by the tail-probability bound provided in
Lemma J.1 of ([Huang et al., 2025](#bib.bib13)). which ensures that the
probability of clipping violations is sufficiently small, of order at
most $`\rho/(n|\Pi|)`$. With this selection, we conclude that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0},\pi_{0}}\!\Big[\big(\Delta^{\pi_{1}}-\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}\big)^{2}\mathbb{I}\{|\Delta^{\pi_{1}}|\leq B_{n,\rho},\;|\Delta^{\pi_{1,\beta}^{*}}|\leq B_{n,\rho}\}\Big]\;\lesssim\;\frac{B_{n,\rho}^{\,2}\log(|\Pi|/\rho)}{n}.
``` |  | (38) |

This completes the transition from the empirical ERM guarantee to a
high-probability population-level error bound.

### C.5 Finalizing the Performance Gap and Deriving the Suboptimality Bound

In this concluding part of the single-step analysis, we integrate the
statistical error bound derived in the previous section with the main
performance gap inequality. This synthesis yields a concrete,
high-probability guarantee on the policy’s performance, which we then
translate into a bound on the probability mass that the policy assigns
to the optimal actions.

##### Simplifying the Performance Gap Bound.

We begin by substituting the statistical error term
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}`$ from
Eq. [38](#A3.E38 "Equation 38 ‣ A Uniform Convergence Bound via Bernstein’s Inequality. ‣ C.4 From Empirical Risk Minimization to a Population-Level Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
into the performance gap inequality
Eq. [31](#A3.Ex54 "In Putting the pieces together. ‣ C.3 Bounding the Difference of Reward Deltas via Region Decomposition ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").
This yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\lesssim\;\big(C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}+C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2}\big)\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}\;+\;\big(C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}^{1/2}+C_{\pi_{1}}^{1/2}\big)\log(C_{\mathrm{loss}})\,\rho^{1/4}\;+\;\beta D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0}).
``` |  | (39) |

To simplify this expression, we upper bound the policy-dependent
coefficients with problem-level constants. Specifically, by Lemma 4.1 in
([Huang et al., 2025](#bib.bib13)), we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}\leq\kappa_{0},\qquad C_{\pi_{1}}\leq\alpha\kappa_{0}.
``` |  |

Moreover, we control the KL divergence by the logarithm of the
concentrability coefficient:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{1}^{*}\|\pi_{0})\;\leq\;\log C_{\pi_{1}^{*}}\;\leq\;\log\kappa_{0}.
``` |  |

Substituting these bounds gives

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\lesssim\;\big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}+\alpha\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\big)\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}\;+\;\big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}+\alpha\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\big)\log(C_{\mathrm{loss}})\,\rho^{1/4}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0}).
``` |  |

For clarity, and because $`\alpha\kappa_{0}`$ is typically of the same
order as $`\kappa_{0}`$ in single-step analysis, we further simplify to

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\lesssim\;\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}\;+\;\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\log(C_{\mathrm{loss}})\,\rho^{1/4}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0}).
``` |  |

To obtain a cleaner form, we balance the statistical error term and the
tail-event term by setting

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\rho^{\prime}:=\rho\wedge\bigg(\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}}{\log C_{\mathrm{loss}}}\bigg)^{4}.
``` |  |

This ensures that the second term does not dominate the first. The
inequality then reduces to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})\;\lesssim\;\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0}).
``` |  | (40) |

##### Bounding the Suboptimality Probability.

We now translate this bound into a high-probability guarantee on the
probability mass assigned to optimal actions. Substituting the above
inequality into
Lemma [C.1](#A3.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma C.1. ‣ C.1 From Performance Gap to Suboptimality Probability. ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
(see also
Eq. [8](#A3.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ Lemma C.1. ‣ C.1 From Performance Gap to Suboptimality Probability. ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{J_{0}(\pi_{1}^{*})-J_{0}(\pi_{1})}{\gamma\,\delta}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).`$ |  | (41) |

Recalling that the statistical error satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{stat}}^{2}\;\lesssim\;\frac{B_{n,\rho}^{2}\log(|\Pi|/\rho)}{n},\qquad B_{n,\rho}\;\asymp\;\log\!\big(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1}\big),
``` |  |

we conclude

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\,\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})\,n^{-1/2}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).
``` |  | (42) |

This provides a direct guarantee on the frequency with which the learned
policy under-weights the oracle’s optimal action.

##### Extending the Bound to the Policy’s Own Optimal Action.

For practical purposes, it is natural to bound the event in which the
learned policy’s *own* maximizer receives insufficient probability mass.
Define

|     |                                                           |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       y_{1}^{*}(x):=\arg\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{1}(y\mid x).
       ```                                                        |     |

By construction,

|     |                                                                 |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\;\geq\;\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x).
       ```                                                              |     |

Hence, whenever the event
$`\{\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\}`$ occurs, it must also be
the case that $`\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta`$. In
set-theoretic form,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\{x:\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\}\;\subseteq\;\{x:\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\}.
``` |  |

Taking probabilities and applying
Eq. [42](#A3.E42 "Equation 42 ‣ Bounding the Suboptimality Probability. ‣ C.5 Finalizing the Performance Gap and Deriving the Suboptimality Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]\;`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\;\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{1}(y_{0}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\,\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})\,n^{-1/2}\;+\;\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).`$ |  | (43) |

This completes the one-step analysis. The derived inequality provides a
high-probability guarantee that the learned policy $`\pi_{1}`$ assigns
sufficient mass to its own optimal actions, with explicit dependence on
the sample size $`n`$, the policy class size $`|\Pi|`$, and the policy
condition number $`\kappa_{0}`$.

### C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number

The analysis so far has provided a crucial single-step guarantee on the
policy’s suboptimality. However, to understand the long-term behavior of
the self-rewarding process, it is essential to move beyond a single
iteration and demonstrate that the policy’s quality, as measured by the
policy condition number, does not degrade over successive generations of
training. An uncontrolled growth in the policy condition number would
signify *model collapse*, where the model becomes increasingly narrow
and overconfident in its own outputs, thereby losing its ability to
generalize. The central argument of our framework rests on proving that
the policy condition number at one step is controlled by, and does not
grow excessively relative to, that of the previous step. This stability
property is formalized in the following lemma.

###### Lemma C.3 (One-Step Policy Condition Number Recurrence).

Let $`\kappa_{t-1}`$ denote the policy condition number at step $`t-1`$.
Assume that for any policy $`\pi_{t}`$, the probabilities assigned to
any action are uniformly lower-bounded by a constant $`c>0`$. Then, the
policy condition number at step $`t`$, denoted $`\kappa_{t}`$, satisfies
the recursive inequality

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{t}\;\lesssim\;1+\frac{1}{c}+\Big(1+\frac{1}{c}\Big)\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}+\Big(1+\frac{1}{c}\Big)\frac{\beta}{\gamma\delta}\log\!\big(\kappa_{t-1}\big).`$ |  | (44) |

###### Proof.

We illustrate the argument for the inductive step from $`t=0`$ to
$`t=1`$; the general case follows by re-indexing.

#### C.6.1 Tail-integral representation.

We recall that the policy condition number is defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{1}(y^{*}_{1}(x)\mid x)}\right],
``` |  | (45) |

where

|     |                                                           |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       y_{1}^{*}(x):=\arg\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{1}(y\mid x).
       ```                                                        |     |

Introducing the random variable

|     |                                              |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Z(x):=\frac{1}{\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)},
       ```                                           |     |

we see that $`\kappa_{1}=\mathbb{E}[Z(x)]`$. A useful way to analyze
such expectations is through the tail-integral (or layer-cake)
representation:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[Z(x)]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big(Z(x)\geq t\big)\,dt.
``` |  | (46) |

By assumption, the policy assigns at least $`c>0`$ probability to every
action, i.e. $`\pi_{1}(y\mid x)\geq c`$ for all $`(x,y)`$. Consequently,
$`Z(x)`$ is bounded:

|     |                                   |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       1\;\leq\;Z(x)\;\leq\;\frac{1}{c}.
       ```                                |     |

This bounded support implies the following trivial bounds on the tail
probability:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}\bigl(Z(x)\geq t\bigr)=\begin{cases}1,&\quad 0\;\leq\;t\;<\;1,\\[6.0pt]
0,&\quad t\;>\;\frac{1}{c}.\end{cases}
``` |  |

Substituting these observations into
Eq. [123](#A6.E123 "Equation 123 ‣ F.5 Bounding the Policy Condition Number 𝜅_1 ‣ Appendix F Proof of Theorem : Performance Guarantees for Linear Softmax Models ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we split the expectation into three parts:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z(x)]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\!\underbrace{\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)}_{=1}\,dt+\int_{1}^{1/c}\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)\,dt+\int_{1/c}^{\infty}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)}_{=0}\,dt`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=1+\int_{1}^{1/c}\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)\,dt.`$ |  | (47) |

Thus, the analysis of $`\kappa_{1}`$ reduces to bounding the integral of
the non-trivial tail probabilities over the interval $`[1,1/c]`$.

#### C.6.2 Relating the tail to suboptimality.

For $`t\geq 1`$, the event $`\{Z(x)\geq t\}`$ is equivalent to

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq\frac{1}{t}.
       ```                                          |     |

Writing $`\delta=1-\frac{1}{t}`$, this becomes
$`\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta`$. From
Eq. [43](#A3.E43 "Equation 43 ‣ Extending the Bound to the Policy’s Own Optimal Action. ‣ C.5 Finalizing the Performance Gap and Deriving the Suboptimality Bound ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we know

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big(\pi_{1}(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big)\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).
``` |  | (48) |

Substituting $`\delta=1-\frac{1}{t}`$ gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)\;\lesssim\;\frac{t}{t-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).
``` |  | (49) |

Let

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A:=\frac{1}{\gamma}\Big(\kappa_{0}^{1/2}\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\Big).
``` |  |

Then

|     |                                                   |     |      |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)\;\lesssim\;\frac{A}{t-1}t.
       ```                                                |     | (50) |

To respect probability upper bounds, we refine this as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}(Z(x)\geq t)\;\leq\;\min\Big\{1,\,\frac{A}{t-1}t\Big\}.
``` |  | (51) |

#### C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number

Then, We provide a complete and self–contained derivation of the
integral that upper–bounds the one–step policy condition number
$`\kappa_{1}`$. As shown earlier, defining
$`Z(x):=1/\pi_{1}\!\big(y_{1}^{*}(x)\mid x\big)`$ and using the
tail–integral identity yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}\;=\;\mathbb{E}[Z(x)]\;\leq\;1+\int_{1}^{\frac{1}{c}}\min\bigl\{1,\;B(t)\bigr\}\,dt,\qquad B(t)\ :=\ A\,\frac{t}{t-1},
``` |  | (52) |

where $`c\in(0,1]`$ is the uniform lower bound on action probabilities
and $`A\in(0,1)`$ is the parameter that emerges from the suboptimality
bound (its explicit form is given in the main text; here we only use
that $`A<1`$). The remainder of the argument is a careful evaluation of
the integral in
Eq. [52](#A3.E52 "Equation 52 ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").

##### Step 1: Identifying the transition point.

The integrand $`\min\{1,B(t)\}`$ switches behavior at the unique
$`t_{0}>1`$ solving $`B(t_{0})=1`$. Solving

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A\,\frac{t_{0}}{t_{0}-1}=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad At_{0}=t_{0}-1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad t_{0}(1-A)=1,
``` |  |

we obtain the explicit transition point

|     |                      |     |      |
|-----|----------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       t_{0}=\frac{1}{1-A},
       ```                   |     | (53) |

which is well defined under the nontrivial regime $`A<1`$. For
$`t\in[1,t_{0}]`$ we have $`B(t)\geq 1`$ and hence $`\min\{1,B(t)\}=1`$,
while for $`t\in[t_{0},1/c]`$ we have $`B(t)\leq 1`$ and hence
$`\min\{1,B(t)\}=B(t)`$.

##### Step 2: Decomposing the integral.

Using the transition point
Eq. [130](#A6.E130 "Equation 130 ‣ 3. Exact integral evaluation via the switch point 𝑡_0. ‣ F.5 Bounding the Policy Condition Number 𝜅_1 ‣ Appendix F Proof of Theorem : Performance Guarantees for Linear Softmax Models ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we split

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\int_{1}^{\frac{1}{c}}\min\{1,B(t)\}\,dt=\int_{1}^{t_{0}}1\,dt\;+\;\int_{t_{0}}^{\frac{1}{c}}B(t)\,dt=(t_{0}-1)\;+\;\int_{t_{0}}^{\frac{1}{c}}A\,\frac{t}{t-1}\,dt.
``` |  | (54) |

The first term evaluates immediately to $`t_{0}-1`$. It remains to
compute the second term.

##### Step 3: Evaluating the second term via an antiderivative.

We simplify the integrand by the identity
$`\frac{t}{t-1}=1+\frac{1}{t-1}`$, and integrate term–wise:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\int\frac{t}{t-1}\,dt=\int\!\Big(1+\frac{1}{t-1}\Big)\,dt=t+\ln|t-1|\;=:\;F_{0}(t).
``` |  |

Therefore,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\frac{1}{c}}A\,\frac{t}{t-1}\,dt`$ | $`\displaystyle=A\big[F_{0}(1/c)-F_{0}(t_{0})\big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=A\Big[\Big(\frac{1}{c}-t_{0}\Big)+\ln\Big(\frac{1/c-1}{t_{0}-1}\Big)\Big],`$ |  |

where we used $`\ln a-\ln b=\ln(a/b)`$ and the fact that $`t_{0}>1`$
ensures $`t_{0}-1>0`$.

##### Step 4: Assembling the bound and simplifying.

Combining the pieces from
Eq. [54](#A3.E54 "Equation 54 ‣ Step 2: Decomposing the integral. ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{1}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 1+(t_{0}-1)+A\Big(\frac{1}{c}-t_{0}\Big)+A\ln\!\Big(\frac{1/c-1}{t_{0}-1}\Big).`$ |  | (55) |

We now substitute $`t_{0}=\frac{1}{1-A}`$ from
Eq. [130](#A6.E130 "Equation 130 ‣ 3. Exact integral evaluation via the switch point 𝑡_0. ‣ F.5 Bounding the Policy Condition Number 𝜅_1 ‣ Appendix F Proof of Theorem : Performance Guarantees for Linear Softmax Models ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").
First, the linear term simplifies to

|     |                                        |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       t_{0}(1-A)\;=\;\frac{1}{1-A}\,(1-A)=1.
       ```                                     |     |

Second, $`t_{0}-1=\frac{1}{1-A}-1=\frac{A}{1-A}`$. Using these
identities in
Eq. [55](#A3.E55 "Equation 55 ‣ Step 4: Assembling the bound and simplifying. ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{1}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{A}{c}+A\ln\!\Big(\frac{(1/c-1)}{A/(1-A)}\Big)=1+\frac{A}{c}+A\ln\!\Big(\frac{(1-c)(1-A)}{A\,c}\Big).`$ |  |

We have thus established the explicit integral evaluation:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}\;\leq\;1+\frac{A}{c}\;+\;A\ln\!\Big(\frac{(1-c)(1-A)}{A\,c}\Big)
``` |  | (56) |

*Interpretation and a relaxed form.* For small $`A`$ (the regime of
interest), $`\ln(1-A)\approx 0`$ and the contribution of $`\ln(1/Ac)`$
dominates. A convenient relaxation is then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}\;\lesssim\;1\;+\;\frac{A}{c}\;+\;A\ln\!\Big(\frac{1}{Ac}\Big)\;\lesssim\;1+\frac{1}{c}+\Big(1+\frac{1}{c}\Big)A,
``` |  |

which is often more transparent in subsequent recursive arguments. (The
exact definition of $`A`$—a function of
$`\gamma,\delta,n,|\Pi|,\rho,\beta,`$ and $`\kappa_{0}`$—is given in the
main text and can be substituted as needed.)

##### Step 5: Final Substitution and the Recursive Relation

We now return to the simplified linearized bound obtained in the
previous section and explicitly substitute the full definition of the
constant $`A`$. Recall that $`A`$ was defined in terms of the
statistical error and the KL regularization component, yielding

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A\;=\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\left(\kappa_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}\;+\;\beta\log\kappa_{0}\right).
``` |  |

Substituting this expression back into the approximate linear coverage
bound, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{1}\;\lesssim\;1+\frac{1}{c}+\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigg)\left[\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\left(\kappa_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta\log(\kappa_{0})\right)\right].
``` |  | (57) |

Expanding the terms inside the brackets distributes the dependence on
the statistical complexity and the KL penalty, leading to a more
transparent form of the recursion:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{1}`$ | $`\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;1+\frac{1}{c}+\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigg)\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}+\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigg)\frac{\beta}{\gamma\delta}\log(\kappa_{0}).`$ |  | (58) |

This recursive inequality explicitly highlights the threefold structure
of the coverage update: (i) a constant offset $`1+1/c`$ due to the
minimal probability assumption, (ii) a term scaling with
$`\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}`$, which reflects the statistical complexity
inherited from the previous step, and (iii) a logarithmic dependence on
$`\kappa_{0}`$ introduced by the KL penalty.

##### Step 6: Extension to Arbitrary Generation Steps by Induction

The derivation so far has been presented for the transition from the
base step ($`t=0`$) to the first update ($`t=1`$). However, the
underlying reasoning does not rely on any special property of these
indices. At a conceptual level, the argument merely exploits two facts:
(i) the tail–integral identity applies uniformly at each step, and (ii)
the suboptimality bound remains valid for every policy $`\pi_{t}`$ with
coverage coefficient $`\kappa_{t}`$.

Consequently, the same sequence of inequalities can be applied
recursively at each generation step. By induction, the bound that
relates $`\kappa_{1}`$ to $`\kappa_{0}`$ extends to a general recurrence
relation between $`\kappa_{t}`$ and $`\kappa_{t-1}`$ for all
$`t\geq 1`$. Formally, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{t}\;\lesssim\;1+\frac{1}{c}+\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigg)\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}+\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigg)\frac{\beta}{\gamma\delta}\log(\kappa_{t-1}).
``` |  | (59) |

##### Interpretation.

This recurrence constitutes a stability guarantee for the policy
condition number. It demonstrates that each new generation of the policy
inherits its complexity from the previous generation in a controlled
manner: the square–root scaling prevents explosive growth, while the
logarithmic correction encapsulates the effect of KL regularization.
Therefore, the recursive inequality
Eq. [59](#A3.E59 "Equation 59 ‣ Step 6: Extension to Arbitrary Generation Steps by Induction ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
forms the theoretical backbone ensuring that the policy condition number
does not diverge across iterations, thereby precluding collapse and
preserving generalization capacity throughout the self-rewarding
process.

∎

### C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number

###### Lemma C.4 (Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number).

Let the sequence of policy condition numbers
$`\{\kappa_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}`$ be governed by the recursive inequality
derived in
Eq. [59](#A3.E59 "Equation 59 ‣ Step 6: Extension to Arbitrary Generation Steps by Induction ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").
Then, for a sufficiently small learning rate parameter $`\beta`$
(specifically, $`\beta\lesssim n^{-1/2}`$), the policy condition number
at generation $`T`$ remains bounded. Moreover, the sequence converges
exponentially fast to a finite stable value, ensuring the stability of
the self-rewarding process. In particular, the explicit asymptotic bound
satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{t}\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{c}+\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{c\gamma\delta}\right)^{2}+\Bigl(1+\sqrt{nc}\Bigr)^{-T}\kappa_{0}.
``` |  | (60) |

###### Proof.

We analyze the recurrence step by step, beginning with a reformulation
and then studying its fixed point and rate of convergence.

##### Step 1: Compact Form of the Recurrence.

Let us define the shorthand

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle M_{0}`$ | $`\displaystyle:=1+\frac{1}{c},`$ |  | (baseline constant) |  | (61) |
|  | $`\displaystyle M_{1}`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigr)\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}},`$ |  | (square-root coefficient) |  | (62) |
|  | $`\displaystyle M_{2}`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigr)\frac{\beta}{\gamma\delta},`$ |  | $`\displaystyle\text{(logarithmic coefficient)}.`$ |  | (63) |

Then the recursive inequality in
Eq. [59](#A3.E59 "Equation 59 ‣ Step 6: Extension to Arbitrary Generation Steps by Induction ‣ C.6.3 Derivation of an Integral Bound on the Policy Condition Number ‣ C.6 Recursive Bound for the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
can be rewritten in the compact form

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{t}\;\leq\;M_{0}+M_{1}\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}+M_{2}\log(\kappa_{t-1}).
``` |  | (64) |

##### Step 2: Bounding the Logarithmic Term.

Since $`\kappa_{t-1}\geq 1`$, we apply the inequality
$`\log x\leq\frac{2}{e}\sqrt{x}`$ for all $`x\geq 1`$. Substituting into
Eq. [64](#A3.E64 "Equation 64 ‣ Step 1: Compact Form of the Recurrence. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
yields

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{t}`$ | $`\displaystyle\;\leq\;M_{0}+M_{1}\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}+\frac{2M_{2}}{e}\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\;\leq\;M_{0}+\Bigl(M_{1}+\frac{2M_{2}}{e}\Bigr)\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}.`$ |  | (65) |

Let $`K:=M_{1}+\frac{2M_{2}}{e}`$, so that the recurrence simplifies to

|     |                                               |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \kappa_{t}\;\leq\;M_{0}+K\sqrt{\kappa_{t-1}}.
       ```                                            |     | (66) |

This gives a cleaner functional recurrence governed by

|     |                                  |     |
|-----|----------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       h(\kappa):=M_{0}+K\sqrt{\kappa}.
       ```                               |     |

##### Step 3: Fixed Point of the Recurrence.

The asymptotic behavior is determined by the fixed point $`U`$ of $`h`$,
which satisfies

|     |                    |     |      |
|-----|--------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       U=M_{0}+K\sqrt{U}.
       ```                 |     | (67) |

Setting $`y=\sqrt{U}`$ yields the quadratic equation

|     |                   |     |
|-----|-------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       y^{2}-Ky-M_{0}=0.
       ```                |     |

By the quadratic formula,

|     |                                      |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       y=\frac{K\pm\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}{2}.
       ```                                   |     |

Since $`y=\sqrt{U}>0`$, we choose the positive solution. Hence,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sqrt{U}=\frac{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}{2},\quad U=\left(\frac{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}{2}\right)^{2}.
``` |  | (68) |

##### Step 4: Convergence Rate via Contraction.

We now analyze the rate at which the recurrence converges to its fixed
point $`U`$. Recall that the recurrence is governed by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa^{\prime}_{t}=h(\kappa^{\prime}_{t-1}),\quad\text{with }h(\kappa)=M_{0}+K\sqrt{\kappa}.
``` |  |

Since $`h`$ is monotone increasing and satisfies $`h(\kappa)<\kappa`$
for all $`\kappa>U`$, the sequence $`\{\kappa^{\prime}_{t}\}`$, when
initialized from $`c>U`$, is monotonically decreasing and bounded below
by $`U`$. This ensures that $`\{\kappa^{\prime}_{t}\}`$ converges to
$`U`$.

To quantify the convergence rate, we invoke the Mean Value Theorem. For
any $`\kappa>U`$, there exists some $`\xi\in(U,\kappa)`$ such that

|     |                                                               |     |      |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       |h(\kappa)-U|=|h(\kappa)-h(U)|=|h^{\prime}(\xi)|\,|\kappa-U|.
       ```                                                            |     | (69) |

The derivative of $`h`$ is given by

|     |                                              |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       h^{\prime}(\kappa)=\frac{K}{2\sqrt{\kappa}}.
       ```                                           |     |

Since $`h^{\prime}(\kappa)`$ is a decreasing function of $`\kappa`$, its
supremum on the interval $`[U,\infty)`$ is attained at $`\kappa=U`$.
Hence, the contraction factor is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
q:=\sup_{\kappa\geq U}h^{\prime}(\kappa)=h^{\prime}(\kappa)=\frac{K}{2\sqrt{U}}.
``` |  | (70) |

Substituting the explicit expression of $`U`$ from
Eq. [68](#A3.E68 "Equation 68 ‣ Step 3: Fixed Point of the Recurrence. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we obtain

|     |                                                      |     |      |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle q=\frac{K}{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}.`$ |     | (71) |

It is immediate that $`0<q<1`$, which confirms that $`h`$ is a
contraction mapping on $`[U,\infty)`$. Consequently, the sequence
$`\{\kappa^{\prime}_{t}\}`$ converges geometrically to its fixed point
$`U`$.

Unrolling the recurrence, we arrive at the inequality

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\kappa^{\prime}_{t}-U|\leq q|\kappa^{\prime}_{t-1}-U|\quad\implies\quad|\kappa^{\prime}_{T}-U|\leq q^{T}|c-U|,
``` |  | (72) |

which provides an explicit bound on the deviation of
$`\kappa^{\prime}_{T}`$ from its fixed point $`U`$. This establishes not
only convergence but also the geometric rate of convergence governed by
the contraction factor $`q`$.

##### Step 5: Explicit Upper Bound for $`\kappa_{t}`$.

Since the original sequence $`\{\kappa_{t}\}`$ is pointwise dominated by
the auxiliary sequence $`\{\kappa^{\prime}_{t}\}`$, we may use the
contraction analysis of Step 4 to bound $`\kappa_{t}`$. Because
$`\{\kappa^{\prime}_{t}\}`$ is monotonically decreasing from its
initialization $`\kappa^{\prime}_{0}=\kappa_{0}`$ towards the fixed
point $`U`$, we have

|     |                                                |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \kappa^{\prime}_{T}-U=|\kappa^{\prime}_{T}-U|.
       ```                                             |     |

Therefore, an explicit upper bound for the original policy condition
number after $`T`$ generations is

|     |                                     |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \,\kappa_{t}\;\leq\;U+q^{T}(c-U)\,,
       ```                                  |     | (73) |

where $`U`$ is the asymptotic fixed point defined in
Eq. [68](#A3.E68 "Equation 68 ‣ Step 3: Fixed Point of the Recurrence. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
and $`q`$ is the geometric contraction factor derived in
Eq. [71](#A3.E71 "Equation 71 ‣ Step 4: Convergence Rate via Contraction. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").

This inequality shows that even if the initial coverage $`\kappa_{0}`$
is large, the sequence converges exponentially fast towards a finite
stable value $`U`$. Thus the self-rewarding process is inherently stable
and avoids uncontrolled growth of the policy condition number.

Expanding the bound more explicitly, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{T}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq U+q^{T}(\kappa_{0}-U)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq(1-q^{T})U+q^{T}\kappa_{0}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\left(\frac{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{K}{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}\right)^{T}\kappa_{0},`$ |  | (74) |

where we have substituted the closed-form expressions for $`U`$ and
$`q`$.

To further interpret this result, we approximate the asymptotic bound
$`U`$ for large $`n`$. Recalling that $`K\approx M_{1}+M_{2}`$, and
ignoring higher-order cross-terms, we obtain

|     |                                        |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       U\;\approx\;M_{0}+M_{1}^{2}+M_{2}^{2}.
       ```                                     |     | (75) |

Hence the recursive bound simplifies to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{T}\;\lesssim\;M_{0}+M_{1}^{2}+M_{2}^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{M_{0}/(M_{1}^{2}+M_{2}^{2})}}\right)^{T}\kappa_{0}.
``` |  | (76) |

Equation Eq. [76](#A3.E76 "Equation 76 ‣ Step 5: Explicit Upper Bound for 𝜅_𝑡. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
makes explicit the decomposition of the bound: the terms $`M_{0}`$,
$`M_{1}^{2}`$, and $`M_{2}^{2}`$ determine the asymptotic ceiling, while
the multiplicative factor $`q^{T}`$ ensures exponential decay of the
dependence on the initial coverage $`\kappa_{0}`$. This completes the
derivation of the explicit upper bound for $`\kappa_{t}`$.

##### Step 6: Scaling Analysis.

Choosing $`\beta\lesssim n^{-1/2}`$ balances the scaling of $`M_{1}`$
and $`M_{2}`$, giving

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
U\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{c}+\frac{1}{n}\Biggl(\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{c\gamma\delta}\Biggr)^{2},
``` |  |

and the contraction factor simplifies to

|     |                                                                    |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       q=\frac{K}{K+\sqrt{K^{2}+4M_{0}}}\;\approx\;\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{nc}}.
       ```                                                                 |     |

Hence,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\kappa_{t}\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{c}+\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{c\gamma\delta}\right)^{2}+\Bigl(1+\sqrt{nc}\Bigr)^{-T}\kappa_{0}.
``` |  | (77) |

This establishes both the boundedness and exponential convergence of the
policy condition number sequence. ∎

### C.8 Conclusion of the Proof: Bounding the Final Policy’s Tail Probability.

Having established in Lemma
[C.4](#A3.Thmtheorem4 "Lemma C.4 (Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number). ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
that the policy condition number $`\kappa_{t}`$ is asymptotically stable
and converges exponentially to a finite upper bound, we now translate
this stability result into a final guarantee on the predictive
confidence of the learned policy. Specifically, we seek to bound the
probability that the policy $`\pi_{T}`$ assigns insufficient mass to its
own optimal response $`y_{T}^{*}(x)`$ after $`T`$ self-rewarding
iterations.

From the preceding derivation, we have for any $`T\geq 1`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{T}(y^{*}_{T}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\left(\kappa_{t-1}^{1/2}\,\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}+\beta\log(\kappa_{t-1})\right).
``` |  | (78) |

The key step is to substitute the asymptotic bound for $`\kappa_{t-1}`$
obtained in
Eq. [77](#A3.E77 "Equation 77 ‣ Step 6: Scaling Analysis. ‣ C.7 Asymptotic Stability of the Policy Condition Number ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models")
into this inequality. Recall that the policy condition number decomposes
into a *stable part*, dominated by $`1/c`$ for large $`n`$, and a
*transient part* that decays exponentially with $`T-1`$.

Step 1 (Square-root term). Applying the inequality
$`(a+b)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}`$ to the decomposition of
$`\kappa_{t-1}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\kappa_{t-1}^{1/2}`$ | $`\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;\left(\frac{1}{c}+\frac{1}{n}\Big(\frac{\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta}\Big)^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\left((1+\sqrt{nc})^{-(T-1)}\kappa_{0}\right)^{1/2}`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}\;+\;(1+\sqrt{nc})^{-\frac{T-1}{2}}\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}.`$ |  | (79) |

For sufficiently large $`n`$, the second-order term
$`\frac{1}{n}(\cdot)^{2}`$ is negligible compared to $`1/c`$.

Step 2 (Logarithmic term). Since $`\kappa_{t-1}`$ is bounded above by a
finite constant $`U`$, its logarithm is also bounded. That is,

|     |                                    |     |
|-----|------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \log(\kappa_{t-1})\;\leq\;\log(U).
       ```                                 |     |

With our choice of $`\beta\lesssim n^{-1/2}`$, the term
$`\beta\log(\kappa_{t-1})`$ is $`\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})`$ and therefore
of the same asymptotic order as the square-root term.

Step 3 (Final substitution). Plugging these bounds back into
Eq. [78](#A3.E78 "Equation 78 ‣ C.8 Conclusion of the Proof: Bounding the Final Policy’s Tail Probability. ‣ Appendix C Proof of Theorem : Finite-Sample Guarantee for Iterative Self-Rewarding Alignment ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"),
we obtain

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\!\big[\pi_{T}(y^{*}_{T}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta\big]`$ | $`\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\gamma\delta}\left[\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}+(1+\sqrt{nc})^{-\frac{T-1}{2}}\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{nc}}\;+\;\frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}}\,(1+\sqrt{nc})^{-\frac{T-1}{2}}\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}.`$ |  | (80) |

Interpretation. The bound consists of two components:

- •
  A *stable error term*,

  |     |                                                      |     |
  |-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|
  |     |
         ``` math
         \frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{nc}},
         ```                                                   |     |

  which vanishes at the parametric rate $`O(n^{-1/2})`$, demonstrating
  that the final policy becomes increasingly confident with more data.
- •
  A *transient error term*,

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \frac{\log(n|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\gamma\delta\sqrt{n}}(1+\sqrt{nc})^{-\frac{T-1}{2}}\sqrt{\kappa_{0}},
  ``` |  |

  which reflects the dependence on the initial coverage $`\kappa_{0}`$
  but decays exponentially fast in $`T`$.

Thus, even if the initial policy is weak (large $`\kappa_{0}`$), its
influence diminishes exponentially across iterations. The long-run
behavior of the self-rewarding process is governed entirely by the
stable error term, ensuring that the final policy allocates sufficient
probability mass to its own optimal predictions.

The proof is complete.

∎

## Appendix D Proof of Theorem [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1 (Single-Step Failure Rate Lower Bound). ‣ 4 Fundamental Limitations of Single-Step Self-Rewarding ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models"): Lower Bound on Single-Step Failure Rate.

Before proceeding to analyze the behavior of a full, multi-step
iterative self-rewarding process, it is essential to first understand
the fundamental limitations inherent in the mechanism itself. This
section is dedicated to exploring these limits by analyzing the
performance of a self-rewarding algorithm within a fixed query budget,
$`n`$. This scenario is equivalent to a single, non-iterative
application of the process.

Our objective is to derive a lower bound on the failure rate that any
algorithm must incur on some challenging problem instance. The proof
proceeds via an adversarial approach: we will construct a class of
problem instances designed to be maximally difficult, thereby probing
for potential failure modes.

The central finding of this analysis will be that an algorithm’s success
is critically dependent on the quality of the initial base model, as
quantified by its policy condition number, $`\kappa_{0}`$. We will
demonstrate that a large initial $`\kappa_{0}`$ (representing a poor
starting model) can lead to a high probability of failure, a scenario
termed model collapse. This result is not a critique of a specific
algorithm, but rather a revelation of an intrinsic risk. The
vulnerability exposed in this section provides the primary motivation
for the analysis in the subsequent sections, where we will investigate
how an iterative framework can overcome this limitation and guide the
process towards a stable and successful outcome.

###### Proof of Theorem [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1 (Single-Step Failure Rate Lower Bound). ‣ 4 Fundamental Limitations of Single-Step Self-Rewarding ‣ Why Self-Rewarding Works: Theoretical Guarantees forIterative Alignment of Language Models").

Our strategy is to establish a necessary condition on the sample size
$`n`$ required for any algorithm to achieve a given success rate. Let an
arbitrary algorithm, after making at most $`n`$ oracle calls, produce a
new policy. We fix a target failure level $`\epsilon`$. We will
construct a specific hard problem instance and demonstrate that if the
algorithm achieves an expected success rate of at least $`1-\epsilon`$
on this instance, then its sample budget $`n`$ must necessarily exceed a
certain threshold.

By inverting this relationship, we establish the core result: for a
fixed budget $`n`$, there exists a worst-case scenario where the failure
rate $`\epsilon`$ must be at least as large as the value dictated by
this threshold. This provides the lower bound on the algorithm’s risk.

In this section we *fix* the query budget $`n`$ (the total number of
sample-and-evaluate calls the algorithm may use), and we derive a *lower
bound on the failure rate* $`\varepsilon`$ that any algorithm must incur
on some instance from $`\Pi`$. The proof proceeds in six explicit steps.

### Step 1: Construction of a Hard Problem Instance.

To establish the lower bound, we construct a problem instance designed
to be maximally challenging for any sharpening algorithm. The
construction begins by defining the spaces for prompts and responses,
followed by the strategic design of the data distribution and the model
class itself.

Let the prompt space be a discrete set
$`\mathcal{X}=\{x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}`$ and the response space be
$`\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{0},y_{1},\ldots,y_{M}\}`$. We fix a margin parameter
$`\gamma\in(0,1)`$ (which we will later set to $`\gamma=\tfrac{1}{2}`$)
and an information-sparsity parameter $`\Delta\in(0,1)`$.

First, we define a prompt distribution $`\mu`$ that concentrates most of
its mass on a single, uninformative point:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mu\;:=\;(1-\Delta)\,\delta_{x_{0}}\;+\;\frac{\Delta}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{x_{i}},`$ |  | (81) |

where $`\delta_{x}`$ denotes the Dirac delta distribution on element
$`x`$. This construction serves a dual purpose. The atom at $`x_{0}`$,
carrying the majority of the probability mass ($`1-\Delta`$), acts as an
uninformative sentinel; an algorithm will frequently sample prompts from
this region but will learn nothing to distinguish between the candidate
models. Conversely, all distinguishing information resides in the small
$`\Delta`$-fraction of the distribution mass spread across the
“informative” points $`\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}`$. As will be shown later,
this sparsity, controlled by $`\Delta`$, is instrumental in amplifying
the overall failure rate from a more fundamental classification error.

Next, we construct a family of response distributions
$`(P_{0},P_{1},\ldots,P_{M})`$ on $`\mathcal{Y}`$, designed to be
difficult to distinguish from one another. The distribution for the
uninformative prompt is deterministic:

|     |                                             |     |      |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle P_{0}\;=\;\delta_{y_{0}}.`$ |     | (82) |

For the informative prompts, we define a set of nearly uniform
distributions, each with a subtle bias towards a specific response:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall\,i\geq 1:\quad P_{i}\;=\;\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)M}\,\delta_{y_{i}}\;+\;\sum_{j\in[M]\setminus\{i\}}\frac{1}{M}\!\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{(M-1)(1-\gamma)}\right)\delta_{y_{j}}.`$ |  | (83) |

For each $`i\geq 1`$, the distribution $`P_{i}`$ is constructed by
taking the uniform distribution over $`n`$ elements and slightly
increasing the probability mass of a single element $`y_{i}`$, while
uniformly decreasing the mass of all other $`M-1`$ elements. The
parameter $`\gamma`$ controls the margin by which $`y_{i}`$ is favored,
making it the unique but only marginally most probable outcome. For
instance, when $`\gamma=\tfrac{1}{2}`$, the probabilities are
$`P_{i}(y_{i})=2/M`$ and $`P_{i}(y_{j})=\frac{M-2}{M(M-1)}`$ for
$`j\neq i`$, highlighting that the distinguishing signal is subtle. This
construction ensures that a large number of samples are required to
reliably detect this statistical bias.

Finally, we synthesize these components to define the full model class.
For any index vector $`\mathcal{I}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in[M]^{d}`$,
which serves as a hidden ”secret key,” we define the corresponding base
model $`\pi^{\mathcal{I}}`$ as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi^{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot\mid x_{i})\;=\;\begin{cases}P_{0},&i=0,\\[2.84526pt]
P_{j_{i}},&i>0.\end{cases}`$ |  | (84) |

The model class $`\Pi`$ is then the set of all such models generated by
every possible secret key:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Pi\;:=\;\{\pi^{\mathcal{I}}:\ \mathcal{I}\in[M]^{d}\},\qquad\text{which implies}\qquad\log|\Pi|\;=\;d\log M.`$ |  | (85) |

Through this construction, the task is effectively reduced to a set of
$`d`$ independent $`n`$-way classification problems. Specifically, for
each informative prompt $`x_{i}`$, the algorithm’s goal is to identify
the single hidden “correct” label $`j_{i}`$, as $`y_{j_{i}}`$ is the
unique maximizer under the response distribution $`P_{j_{i}}`$. The
overall difficulty of this task, and thus the reason this construction
is suitable for proving a lower bound, is compounded by two
strategically designed factors.

First, the statistical similarity of the candidate distributions makes
the classification inherently challenging. The distribution
$`P_{j_{i}}`$ is only *slightly* biased toward its maximizer
$`y_{j_{i}}`$, meaning a large number of samples at prompt $`x_{i}`$ are
required to reliably detect this subtle statistical bump. With a limited
sample budget, many of the hidden labels $`j_{i}`$ will therefore remain
ambiguous to the algorithm. Second, an informational scarcity is
enforced by the prompt distribution $`\mu`$. The heavy probability mass
concentrated at the uninformative prompt $`x_{0}`$ (a proportion of
$`1-\Delta`$) further dilutes the evidence available to the learner, as
most samples drawn will provide no information about any of the hidden
labels $`j_{i}`$.

Together, these two effects—the need for many samples to resolve
ambiguity at each informative prompt and the rarity of encountering such
prompts—create the challenging family of models necessary to prove a
tight lower bound on sample complexity.

### Step 2: Tune $`\Delta`$ via an Inlined Reduction-To-Classification Argument.

Fix an arbitrary algorithm and let $`\widehat{\pi}`$ be the model it
outputs using at most $`n`$ samples. For a target failure level
$`\varepsilon\in(0,1)`$ we set

|     |                              |     |
|-----|------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Delta\;:=\;16\,\varepsilon.
       ```                           |     |

(Here $`\Delta`$ is part of the adversarially chosen prompt distribution
$`\mu`$ from Step 1.) We now show that if the algorithm achieved overall
success at least $`1-\varepsilon`$, then the average misclassification
rate on the $`d`$ informative prompts must be at most
$`\varepsilon/\Delta=1/16`$.

Success event and its consequence at a fixed informative prompt. Recall
from Step 1 that for each $`i\in[d]`$ the maximizer set is a singleton,
$`y^{\mathcal{I}}(x_{i})=\{y_{j_{i}^{\star}}\}`$, where
$`j_{i}^{\star}`$ is the unique index of the favored label at $`x_{i}`$.
Define the success event at $`x`$ by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{S}(x)\;:=\;\Bigl\{\widehat{\pi}\bigl(y^{\mathcal{I}}(x)\mid x\bigr)>\tfrac{1}{2}\Bigr\}.
``` |  |

For $`i\in[d]`$ let
$`\widehat{j}_{i}:=\arg\max_{j\in[M]}\widehat{\pi}(y_{j}\mid x_{i})`$ be
the algorithm’s predicted label at $`x_{i}`$. Because
$`y^{\mathcal{I}}(x_{i})=\{y_{j_{i}^{\star}}\}`$ and
$`\widehat{\pi}(y_{j_{i}^{\star}}\mid x_{i})>\tfrac{1}{2}`$ on
$`\mathcal{S}(x_{i})`$, the maximizer must coincide with the true index:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{S}(x_{i})\ \Longrightarrow\ \widehat{j}_{i}=j_{i}^{\star}.
``` |  |

Equivalently, using indicator notation,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\ \leq\ \mathbb{I}\bigl\{\neg\mathcal{S}(x_{i})\bigr\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbb{P}\bigl[\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\bigr]\ \leq\ 1-\mathbb{P}\bigl[\mathcal{S}(x_{i})\bigr].
``` |  |

From overall success under $`\mu`$ to per-coordinate success. All
probabilities and expectations below are taken over the internal
randomness of the algorithm, the data it observes on instance
````
